[2026-09-18T11:37|Ugur Keser|72215] DBBI is the first 91 characters of the SalPhaseIon stream, drawn from a nine-letter alphabet a–i. You can't parse it on its own, because the parser's parameters live in the picture: the rabbit image is a 14×14 grid read in spiral order, and every 8th cell is coloured — those colours are the last bit of each byte of the URL gsmg.io/theseedisplanted, blue for 1, yellow for 0. Line those 24 colours up in spiral order, splice in the single off-white FEFEFE cell at spiral position 164 as the 21st symbol, and you have a 25-symbol program. You run it like this: walk logical positions starting at 1; if the position isn't prime you consume one character from the stream and call it filler, and if it is prime you look at the next colour symbol and consume 1 character for blue or 2 for yellow, calling that chunk a "host". There are 23 primes up to 83, eight of them yellow; 83 logical steps plus the 8 extra characters the yellows eat comes to exactly 91, so the stream is consumed precisely — nothing left over, nothing short. And here's the verification: all 23 hosts start with b, and every yellow host is exactly be. On a shuffled DBBI that lands around 6.9/23 on average, and across 200,000 shuffles it never once hits 23/23 — p < 5×10⁻⁶. Drop FEFEFE from the program and you fall to 21/23; treat it as yellow instead of blue and you also get 21/23, so that cell isn't decoration, it's a required part of the program. The upshot is that DBBI is a second copy of the picture's colour sequence written in a completely different encoding; it carries no new payload of its own, it behaves like a checksum telling you that you're holding the right key.

[2026-09-19T12:59|Doober|72286] Cosmic Duality is already an OpenSSL-style salted container. The decoded bytes begin with the literal Salted__, followed by an 8-byte salt and then ciphertext. \n  \n 1. Valid PKCS#7 padding \n A wrong AES-CBC password/key produces plaintext that is effectively random bytes. Random bytes can accidentally have valid PKCS#7 padding. \n For AES's 16-byte block: \n \[ P(\text{valid padding}) = \frac1{256}+\frac1{256^2}+\cdots+\frac1{256^{16}} \approx \frac1{255} \] \n So roughly 0.39% of wrong decryptions, about 1 in 255–256, will look like they have valid padding.
[2026-09-19T13:00|Doober|72287] So if you open it and it doesn't have any discernible understandible text like this puzzle has produced during the solve path, then it's most likely padding.

[2026-09-27T15:09|Андрей Александрович|73793] We got ANTIYBCORE from the DBBI branch like this: \n  \n 1. We mapped the DBBI alphabet by first appearance: \n  \n  \n  \n D=0, B=1, I=2, F=3, H=4, C=5, E=6, G=7, A=8 \n  \n 2. We placed the 91-character DBBI string into its natural 7×13 grid and summed each column. That gives: \n  \n  \n  \n [19, 27, 26, 34, 13, 38, 11, 31, 25, 37, 18, 27, 33] \n  \n 3. We treated those 13 numbers as zero-based “nth word from the end” indices applied to the Architect text immediately before the modified SELECT section. \n  \n  \n  \n That selects these words: \n  \n A / NOW / TO / IT / YOU / BECOME / CARRY / OF / RETURN / EXCEEDINGLY / TEMPORARY / NOW / THE \n  \n 4. Taking the first letter of each selected word gives: \n  \n  \n  \n A N T I Y B C O R E T N T \n  \n So the output is: \n  \n ANTIYBCORETNT \n  \n and the striking prefix is: \n  \n ANTIYBCORE \n  \n This was interesting because ANTI, YB, and CORE all matched structures we were already seeing elsewhere: anti-diagonal geometry, Yellow/Blue, and the 84-cell interior/core. \n  \n Important caveat: the extraction is fully reproducible, and random-shuffle controls made the ANTIYBCORE prefix unusually strong, but it is still not creator-authenticated because the “take initials of the selected words” step is inferred rather than explicitly instructed.

[2026-10-01T15:59|sol|74864] 1.XOR — exclusive OR \n Meaning: Exactly one is true, but not both. \n Example: “You may enter with a ticket XOR a wristband.” \n Ticket only → ✅ yes \n Wristband only → ✅ yes \n Both → ❌ no \n Neither → ❌ no \n So XOR is like: “one or the other, but not both.” \n  \n 2. “and/or” \n Meaning: Inclusive OR. It means one, the other, or both. \n Example: “Bring a pen and/or paper.” \n Pen only → ✅ yes \n Paper only → ✅ yes \n Both pen and paper → ✅ yes \n Neither → ❌ no

[2026-10-10T23:44|Heisenberg|76225] Small clean-up of the dbbi <-> URL link, in case it saves someone time. \n  \n The 23 prime-index tokens of dbbi (b = 2 / be = 25) are the ASCII LSBs of the phase-1 URL ("theseedisplanted") (b = odd, be = even), i.e. the same bits as the 24 coloured cells of the poster spiral (index 8k+7). \n  \n URL parity (24 bits): 111101110011110110010010 (= 0xF73D92) \n 84-token parse: 11110111001111011001101 -> only marker 21 is flipped \n 83-token parse: 11110111001111011001100 -> 0 mismatches if one '1' is inserted after bit 20 \n  \n Either way that's only 1 bit per URL char. I also tried removing the prime tokens and laying out the rest of dbbi in matrices 4-14 wide (parity / threshold / ASCII / A1Z26 readings, all shifts and reversals): the URL never comes back, so the text itself has to come from the poster's black/white bits, not from dbbi.
[2026-10-10T23:45|Heisenberg|76226] For reference, the poster's black/white bits (spiral, 24 bytes + 4 zero bits) decode to this ASCII: \n 103 115 109 103 46 105 111 47 116 104 101 115 101 101 100 105 115 112 108 97 110 116 101 100 \n  \n Last bit of each byte (odd = blue, even = yellow): \n 111101110011110110010010 \n The first 23 of those are the dbbi prime-index tokens.

[2026-10-14T22:05|Mehdi|76694] Well, now I came up with something interesting. \n I think the concept of Yin–Yang and balance can be seen throughout many parts of this story and its puzzles. \n For example, in a part of the puzzle we saw before, where we got: \n Warning logic \n Can yo dig it \n each section originally had two clearly separated colors. At first, the colors were not next to each other, and when the sentence was incorrect, the colors were not paired and there was no balance. But after we solved it correctly, the colors combined into blue/black and red, and we reached a kind of Yin–Yang balance. \n Or, in another part of the puzzle related to chess, the initial position before moving the rook was not balanced. White was in a winning position, but we were asked to move the white rook in a way that would prevent Black from being checkmated. That is why the rook moved to c6. After that move, the chessboard reached a balance between Black and White. \n And then, in The Matrix, we learned that Zion and the Matrix are actually parts of the same system. They need each other for the system to remain balanced, and both are parts of a larger whole. \n That is why, when the previous Ones chose the Source, Zion was destroyed and the Matrix was reset. \n A number of humans were then selected to build a new Zion. \n The cycle started again. \n In other words, the system returned to balance again. \n There is even another example. \n The relationship between Heisenberg's uncertainty principle and Yin–Yang is conceptually very interesting. In fact, one of the founders of quantum mechanics, Niels Bohr, personally used the Yin–Yang symbol to express one of the most important ideas in quantum physics. \n 1. The common idea: two complementary aspects of one reality \n In Yin–Yang philosophy, the universe consists of aspects that appear to be opposites but are actually complementary to each other. \n For example, light and darkness are opposites, but our understanding of each depends on the other. \n In the uncertainty principle, we are also dealing with two properties of the same physical system: \n Position: Where is the particle? \n Momentum: How much momentum does the particle have? \n Heisenberg's relation is: \n [ \Delta x \Delta p \geq \frac{\hbar}{2} ] \n The smaller the uncertainty in position becomes, the larger the minimum possible uncertainty in momentum must become. \n Conceptually, this resembles Yin–Yang: the two aspects belong to the same reality, but they cannot both be brought to perfect precision simultaneously. \n So, throughout many parts of the puzzle, we can see the concept of Yin–Yang and the balance between two halves. \n However, there is one place where this balance does not seem to exist: the first image, or the rabbit. \n There, we have 15 blue cells and 9 yellow cells. \n The total is 24, which means that, to achieve balance, we should have: \n 12 blue + 12 yellow = 24 \n So, three blue cells would have to become yellow. \n This seems to connect to one of the creator's hints, which suggests that some of the ones should become zeros. \n Now the question is: \n Which three blue cells should be changed to yellow? \n Could it be that, according to the creator's hint, the cells located at prime-numbered positions are the ones that should change? \n If so, then the way we number the cells of the matrix becomes important. \n How exactly should we number the matrix? \n And more importantly: \n Is this same kind of imbalance present somewhere else in the puzzle, perhaps in another section that we have not solved yet? Could we be expected to identify that imbalance and bring it back into balance?

[2026-10-15T11:18|Андрей Александрович|76767] We started with the 1327-byte Cosmic Duality output. \n Original method: We split the first 1248 bytes into 39 blocks of 32 bytes (A16 + B16). Taking B16 from each block and appending a 4-byte index, we generated 39 Bitcoin P2PKH addresses using Base58Check. \n That's where 1Bwq9PK... and 1Hby7BY... came from. We derived them from Cosmic itself, not from an external source. \n New hypothesis: The remaining 78 bytes represent 39 two-byte control values. PRIME selects 12 of the 39 blocks, and each control value's parity determines whether to use A16 or B16. \n The final byte, 0x3A (58), might indicate Base58. \n Important: This is still a hypothesis. Generating valid Base58Check addresses does not prove that they are the intended puzzle addresses.

[2026-10-17T20:06|Marc|76980] Hey all — method tip from someone who lurks a lot: \n  \n A valid PKCS7 padding on those blobs is NOT an opening; without a \n null baseline you just collect chance. Same with blind "brute-force \n the ciphertext" loops — calibrate first, then measure. \n  \n GL.
[2026-10-17T20:08|F1f7h|76981] Marc my words,, what you mean with a "null baseline"?
[2026-10-17T20:09|3.14159265358979323846264338327950288419716939937510 0b1one|58209749445923078164062862089986280348253421170679|76982] useless stuff, it could come handy if you fall from a cliff
[2026-10-17T20:12|Marc|76983] A null baseline = run the exact same test on random/control data \n first, and count how often it "hits" by chance. If your result shows \n up about as often on noise as on the real thing, it's just chance. \n  \n Example: ~1 in 256 random AES attempts passes a PKCS7 padding check, \n so getting 1-2 "paddings" means nothing until you compare against \n that rate. Calibrate against the noise, then measure. GL.
